=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given two arrays, nums and target.
In a single operation, you may increment any element of nums by 1.
Return the minimum number of operations required so that each element in target has at least one multiple in nums.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,2,3], target = [4]
Output: 1
Explanation:
The minimum number of operations required to satisfy the condition is 1.

Increment 3 to 4 with just one operation, making 4 a multiple of itself.


Example 2:

Input: nums = [8,4], target = [10,5]
Output: 2
Explanation:
The minimum number of operations required to satisfy the condition is 2.

Increment 8 to 10 with 2 operations, making 10 a multiple of both 5 and 10.


Example 3:

Input: nums = [7,9,10], target = [7]
Output: 0
Explanation:
Target 7 already has a multiple in nums, so no additional operations are needed.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 5 * 10^4
1 <= target.length <= 4
target.length <= nums.length
1 <= nums[i], target[i] <= 10^4

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def minimumIncrements(self, nums: List[int], target: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

    base = dp[mask]
    if base >= INF: continue
    rem = full ^ mask  # bits not covered
    # enumerate submasks s of rem

        if s == 0:
            break
        s = (s - 1) & rem

Let's decide final implementation details:
```
from typing import List
from math import gcd

class Solution:
    def minimumIncrements(self, nums: List[int], target: List[int]) -> int:
        k = len(target)
        full = (1 << k) - 1
        M = 1 << k
        
        lcm = [1] * M
        for mask in range(1, M):
            bit = mask & -mask
            j = bit.bit_length() - 1
            prev = mask ^ bit
            lcm[mask] = lcm[prev] // gcd(lcm[prev], target[j]) * target[j]
        
        # precompute disjoint submasks for each mask
        submasks = [[] for _ in range(M)]
        for mask in range(M):
            rem = full ^ mask
            s = rem
            while True:
                submasks[mask].append(s)
                if s == 0:
                    break
                s = (s - 1) & rem
        
        INF = 10**18
        dp = [INF] * M
        dp[0] = 0
        
        for num in nums:
            cost = [0] * M
            for mask in range(1, M):
                L = lcm[mask]
                cost[mask] = (L - num % L) % L
            # update dp in-place descending
            for mask in range(M - 1, -1, -1):
                base = dp[mask]
                if base >= INF:
                    continue
                # enumerate subsets of remaining targets
                for s in submasks[mask]:
                    nm = mask | s
                    val = base + cost[s]
                    if val < dp[nm]:
                        dp[nm] = val
        return dp[full]
```
Need check if `submasks[mask]` includes s=0, so for each mask, nm=mask, val=base, no change. If base finite. Good.

So nm processed earlier. Good. However, what about if mask=0, rem=full, enumerate s=full first (if using submask enumeration from rem down to 0). It updates dp[full]. full processed earlier. Good. Then s=... lower. No issue.

Potential issue: Because we restrict to disjoint s, but `cost[s]` for s=0 =0. If base finite, val=base. `if val < dp[mask]` false. Fine.

Could we skip `if base >= INF`? If base INF, val INF+cost > INF, not < dp[nm] (INF), so no update. But if cost negative no. So skip optional. But if cost huge and INF+cost maybe > INF, no update. We can skip for speed.

```

subs = submasks
cost_local = cost
for mask in range(M-1,-1,-1):
    base = dp_local[mask]
    if base == INF: continue
    for s in subs[mask]:
        nm = mask | s
        v = base + cost_local[s]
        if v < dp_local[nm]:
            dp_local[nm] = v
```

Our DP can assign all targets that a num covers to it; if final value is multiple of lcm of assigned subset, cost same. If a num covers extra target not in assigned subset, we can add it to subset without increasing cost (because final value already multiple), so there is an optimal representation where each target covered by a num is assigned to one of the nums covering it, and each num's assigned subset is exactly some subset of targets it covers. Let's implement cost_table:
```
max_n = max(nums)
cost_table = [[0] * M for _ in range(max_n + 1)]
for v in range(1, max_n + 1):
    row = cost_table[v]
    for mask in range(1, M):
        L = lcm[mask]
        row[mask] = (L - v % L) % L
```
Then in DP:
```
for num in nums:
    cost = cost_table[num]
    new = dp[:]
    for mask in range(M):
        base = dp[mask]
        if base >= INF:
            continue
        for s, nm in trans[mask]:
            v = base + cost[s]
            if v < new[nm]:
                new[nm] = v
    dp = new
```
This is efficient. But if max(nums) maybe 10000, table 160k. Good. If nums values maybe up to 10^4, yes. If constraints changed, table size maybe max(nums) up to? Problem says <=1e4. Good.

```
from typing import List
from math import gcd

class Solution:
    def minimumIncrements(self, nums: List[int], target: List[int]) -> int:
        k = len(target)
        full = (1 << k) - 1
        M = 1 << k

        lcm = [1] * M
        for mask in range(1, M):
            bit = mask & -mask
            j = bit.bit_length() - 1
            prev = mask ^ bit
            lcm[mask] = lcm[prev] // gcd(lcm[prev], target[j]) * target[j]

        trans = [[] for _ in range(M)]
        for mask in range(M):
            rem = full ^ mask
            s = rem
            while s:
                nm = mask | s
                trans[mask].append((s, nm))
                s = (s - 1) & rem

        max_n = max(nums)
        cost_table = [[0] * M for _ in range(max_n + 1)]
        for v in range(1, max_n + 1):
            row = cost_table[v]
            for mask in range(1, M):
                L = lcm[mask]
                row[mask] = (L - v % L) % L

        INF = 10**30
        dp = [INF] * M
        dp[0] = 0

        for num in nums:
            cost = cost_table[num]
            new = dp[:]
            for mask in range(M):
                base = dp[mask]
                if base >= INF:
                    continue
                for s, nm in trans[mask]:
                    val = base + cost[s]
                    if val < new[nm]:
                        new[nm] = val
            dp = new
            if dp[full] == 0:
                return 0

        return dp[full]
```
Need check if `trans[mask]` includes s nonempty only. For mask=full, rem=0, trans empty. For mask=0, trans includes all nonempty masks. Good.

Is that always true? For a single num, cost to cover subset A is <= cost to cover superset A∪B? Let's check: cost_i(A∪B) = distance to lcm(L_A,L_B). Is it always >= cost_i(A)? Now, maybe if cost is not monotone due to lcm? It is monotone: if A⊆B, L_A divides L_B, next multiple of L_A <= next multiple of L_B? The smallest multiple of L_A >= n is <= smallest multiple of L_B >= n. Thus d_A <= d_B. So cost monotone. Good.

But before final, let's ensure code syntax correct:
```
from typing import List
from math import gcd

class Solution:
    def minimumIncrements(self, nums: List[int], target: List[int]) -> int:
        k = len(target)
        full = (1 << k) - 1
        M = 1 << k

        lcm = [1] * M
        for mask in range(1, M):
            bit = mask & -mask
            j = bit.bit_length() - 1
            prev = mask ^ bit
            lcm[mask] = lcm[prev] // gcd(lcm[prev], target[j]) * target[j]

        trans = [[] for _ in range(M)]
        for mask in range(M):
            rem = full ^ mask
            s = rem
            while s:
                nm = mask | s
                trans[mask].append((s, nm))
                s = (s - 1) & rem

        max_n = max(nums)
        cost_table = [[0] * M for _ in range(max_n + 1)]
        for v in range(1, max_n + 1):
            row = cost_table[v]
            for mask in range(1, M):
                L = lcm[mask]
                row[mask] = (L - v % L) % L

        INF = 10 ** 30
        dp = [INF] * M
        dp[0] = 0

        for num in nums:
            cost = cost_table[num]
            new = dp[:]
            for mask in range(M):
                base = dp[mask]
                if base >= INF:
                    continue
                for s, nm in trans[mask]:
                    val = base + cost[s]
                    if val < new[nm]:
                        new[nm] = val
            dp = new
            if dp[full] == 0:
                return 0

        return dp[full]
```

But if target list length k, and mask has bit j set, target[j] exists. Good.

But note: If lcm is huge, `num % L` with L huge and num small is cheap. 750k is fine. Let's use on-the-fly for clarity. The final code:
```
from typing import List
from math import gcd

class Solution:
    def minimumIncrements(self, nums: List[int], target: List[int]) -> int:
        k = len(target)
        full = (1 << k) - 1
        M = 1 << k

        lcm = [1] * M
        for mask in range(1, M):
            bit = mask & -mask
            j = bit.bit_length() - 1
            prev = mask ^ bit
            lcm[mask] = lcm[prev] // gcd(lcm[prev], target[j]) * target[j]

        trans = [[] for _ in range(M)]
        for mask in range(M):
            rem = full ^ mask
            s = rem
            while s:
                trans[mask].append((s, mask | s))
                s = (s - 1) & rem

        INF = 10**30
        dp = [INF] * M
        dp[0] = 0

        for num in nums:
            cost = [0] * M
            for mask in range(1, M):
                L = lcm[mask]
                cost[mask] = (L - num % L) % L

            new = dp[:]
            for mask in range(M):
                base = dp[mask]
                if base >= INF:
                    continue
                for s, nm in trans[mask]:
                    val = base + cost[s]
                    if val < new[nm]:
                        new[nm] = val
            dp = new
            if dp[full] == 0:
                return 0

        return dp[full]
```
This is concise. Let's test with examples. Good.

Then current num covers s (which includes o and n), final mask (p\o) ∪ s = p ∪ n = p|s. Since s disjoint from p\o? s includes o, and p\o excludes o, so disjoint. Cost dp[p\o]+cost[s] <= dp[p]+cost[s]. Thus disjoint transition from p\o to p|s dominates. Good. So restricting disjoint safe.

Before final, let's consider if there is a possible bug in old-copy DP with disjoint transitions and `trans[mask]` precomputed as nonempty subsets of remaining targets. Suppose old mask p has some targets covered. Current num could cover a subset s that includes targets already covered, and by doing so, it could allow previous nums to cover a different subset that was not covered before? No, previous nums decisions fixed in dp[p]. If current num covers overlap, final mask same; no new targets. It cannot free previous nums to cover other targets because previous nums already processed and their coverage is fixed in dp[p]. But dp[p] is min cost to cover p using previous nums; it doesn't store alternative coverage of other targets. If current num covers some targets in p, then previous nums didn't need to cover them; that would correspond to a different old mask p' (p minus overlap), and previous nums could have covered other targets? Wait previous nums processed before current num; in the actual solution, previous nums might cover a set p' that is not a subset of p? Let's think.

Our DP state dp[p] means "minimum cost using processed nums to cover exactly/at least mask p"? We treat dp[mask] as cost to cover at least mask? Actually we update dp[mask|s] and never remove bits, so dp[mask] is min cost to cover all bits in mask (possibly more? But we store by exact OR; if a solution covers more than mask, it would be stored at larger mask, not at mask. However, dp[mask] could also be achieved by covering extra bits? No, update stores OR result, so if extra bits covered, mask would include them. So dp[mask] is exact mask covered, not at least. But for monotonicity, dp[p'] <= dp[p] if p' subset p? Is that true for exact mask? If a solution covers p exactly, it also covers p' (as a subset) but the cost is same; dp[p'] is min cost to cover exactly p', which could be less or equal because the same solution covers p' (and extra bits p\p'). Wait if the solution covers p exactly, it covers p' plus extra bits. dp[p'] allows covering exactly p', so it can use the same nums and maybe not require extra bits; cost same. Thus dp[p'] <= dp[p]. Good. So overlapping transition dominated.

But consider if previous nums in dp[p] cover p exactly, and current num covers overlap s, final mask p|s = p (if s subset p) or p plus new. Let overlap o = s ∩ p, new bits n = s \ p. The old mask p includes o. If current num covers o and n, then previous nums didn't need to cover o; they could have covered p \ o instead, with cost dp[p \ o] <= dp[p]. Then current num covers s (which includes o and n), final mask (p\o) ∪ s = p ∪ n = p|s. Since s disjoint from p\o? s includes o, and p\o excludes o, so disjoint. Cost dp[p\o]+cost[s] <= dp[p]+cost[s]. Thus disjoint transition from p\o to p|s dominates. Good. So restricting disjoint safe.

Before final, let's consider if there is a possible bug in old-copy DP with disjoint transitions and `trans[mask]` precomputed as nonempty subsets of remaining targets. Suppose old mask p has some targets covered. Current num could cover a subset s that includes targets already covered, and by doing so, it could allow previous nums to cover a different subset that was not covered before? No, previous nums decisions fixed in dp[p]. If current num covers overlap, final mask same; no new targets. It cannot free previous nums to cover other targets because previous nums already processed and their coverage is fixed in dp[p]. But dp[p] is min cost to cover p using previous nums; it doesn't store alternative coverage of other targets. If current num covers some targets in p, then previous nums didn't need to cover them; that would correspond to a different old mask p' (p minus overlap), and previous nums could have covered other targets? Wait previous nums processed before current num; in the actual solution, previous nums might cover a set p' that is not a subset of p? Let's think.

Our DP state dp[p] means "minimum cost using processed nums to cover exactly/at least mask p"? We treat dp[mask] as cost to cover at least mask? Actually we update dp[mask|s] and never remove bits, so dp[mask] is min cost to cover all bits in mask (possibly more? But we store by exact OR; if a solution covers more than mask, it would be stored at larger mask, not at mask. However, dp[mask] could also be achieved by covering extra bits? No, update stores OR result, so if extra bits covered, mask would include them. So dp[mask] is exact mask covered, not at least. But for monotonicity, dp[p'] <= dp[p] if p' subset p? Is that true for exact mask? If a solution covers p exactly, it also covers p' (as a subset) but the cost is same; dp[p'] is min cost to cover exactly p', which could be less or equal because the same solution covers p' (and extra bits p\p'). Wait if the solution covers p exactly, it covers p' plus extra bits. dp[p'] allows covering exactly p', so it can use the same nums and maybe not require extra bits; cost same. Thus dp[p'] <= dp[p]. Good. So overlapping transition dominated.

But consider if previous nums in dp[p] cover p exactly, and current num covers overlap s, final mask p|s = p (if s subset p) or p plus new. Let overlap o = s ∩ p, new bits n = s \ p. The old mask p includes o. If current num covers o and n, then previous nums didn't need to cover o; they could have covered p \ o instead, with cost dp[p \ o] <= dp[p]. Then current num covers s (which includes o and n), final mask (p\o) ∪ s = p ∪ n = p|s. Since s disjoint from p\o? s includes o, and p\o excludes o, so disjoint. Cost dp[p\o]+cost[s] <= dp[p]+cost[s]. Thus disjoint transition from p\o to p|s dominates. Good. So restricting disjoint safe.

It cannot free previous nums to cover other targets because previous nums already processed and their coverage is fixed in dp[p]. But dp[p] is min cost to cover p using previous nums; it doesn't store alternative coverage of other targets. If current num covers some targets in p, then previous nums didn't need to cover them; that would correspond to a different old mask p' (p minus overlap), and previous nums could have covered other targets? Our DP state dp[p] means "minimum cost using processed nums to cover exactly/at least mask p"? We treat dp[mask] as cost to cover at least mask? Actually we update dp[mask|s] and never remove bits, so dp[mask] is min cost to cover all bits in mask (possibly more? But we store by exact OR; if a solution covers more than mask, it would be stored at larger mask, not at mask. However, dp[mask] could also be achieved by covering extra bits? No, update stores OR result, so if extra bits covered, mask would include them. So dp[mask] is exact mask covered, not at least. But for monotonicity, dp[p'] <= dp[p] if p' subset p? Is that true for exact mask? If a solution covers p exactly, it also covers p' (as a subset) but the cost is same; dp[p'] is min cost to cover exactly p', which could be less or equal because the same solution covers p' (and extra bits p\p'). Wait if the solution covers p exactly, it covers p' plus extra bits. dp[p'] allows covering exactly p', so it can use the same nums and maybe not require extra bits; cost same. Thus dp[p'] <= dp[p]. Good. So overlapping transition dominated.

But consider if previous nums in dp[p] cover p exactly, and current num covers overlap s, final mask p|s = p (if s subset p) or p plus new. Let overlap o = s ∩ p, new bits n = s \ p. The old mask p includes o. If current num covers o and n, then previous nums didn't need to cover o; they could have covered p \ o instead, with cost dp[p \ o] <= dp[p]. Then current num covers s (which includes o and n), final mask (p\o) ∪ s = p ∪ n = p|s. Since s disjoint from p\o? s includes o, and p\o excludes o, so disjoint. Cost dp[p\o]+cost[s] <= dp[p]+cost[s]. Thus disjoint transition from p\o to p|s dominates. Good. So restricting disjoint safe.

Before final, let's consider if there is a possible bug in old-copy DP with disjoint transitions and `trans[mask]` precomputed as nonempty subsets of remaining targets. Suppose old mask p has some targets covered. Current num could cover a subset s that includes targets already covered, and by doing so, it could allow previous nums to cover a different subset that was not covered before? No, previous nums decisions fixed in dp[p]. If current num covers overlap, final mask same; no new targets. It cannot free previous nums to cover other targets because previous nums already processed and their coverage is fixed in dp[p]. But dp[p] is min cost to cover p using previous nums; it doesn't store alternative coverage of other targets. If current num covers some targets in p, then previous nums didn't need to cover them; that would correspond to a different old mask p' (p minus overlap), and previous nums could have covered other targets? Wait previous nums processed before current num; in the actual solution, previous nums might cover a set p' that is not a subset of p? Let's think.

Our DP state dp[p] means "minimum cost using processed nums to cover exactly/at least mask p"? We treat dp[mask] as cost to cover at least mask? Actually we update dp[mask|s] and never remove bits, so dp[mask] is min cost to cover all bits in mask (possibly more? But we store by exact OR; if a solution covers more than mask, it would be stored at larger mask, not at mask. However, dp[mask] could also be achieved by covering extra bits? No, update stores OR result, so if extra bits covered, mask would include them. So dp[mask] is exact mask covered, not at least. But for monotonicity, dp[p'] <= dp[p] if p' subset p? Is that true for exact mask? If a solution covers p exactly, it also covers p' (as a subset) but the cost is same; dp[p'] is min cost to cover exactly p', which could be less or equal because the same solution covers p' (and extra bits p\p'). Wait if the solution covers p exactly, it covers p' plus extra bits. dp[p'] allows covering exactly p', so it can use the same nums and maybe not require extra bits; cost same. Thus dp[p'] <= dp[p]. Good. So overlapping transition dominated.

But consider if previous nums in dp[p] cover p exactly, and current num covers overlap s, final mask p|s = p (if s subset p) or p plus new. Let overlap o = s ∩ p, new bits n = s \ p. The old mask p includes o. If current num covers o and n, then previous nums didn't need to cover o; they could have covered p \ o instead, with cost dp[p \ o] <= dp[p]. Then current num covers s (which includes o and n), final mask (p\o) ∪ s = p ∪ n = p|s. Since s disjoint from p\o? s includes o, and p\o excludes o, so disjoint. Cost dp[p\o]+cost[s] <= dp[p]+cost[s]. Thus disjoint transition from p\o to p|s dominates. Good. So restricting disjoint safe.

Before final, let's consider if there is a possible bug in old-copy DP with disjoint transitions and `trans[mask]` precomputed as nonempty subsets of remaining targets. Suppose old mask p has some targets covered. Current num could cover a subset s that includes targets already covered, and by doing so, it could allow previous nums to cover a different subset that was not covered before? No, previous nums decisions fixed in dp[p]. If current num covers overlap, final mask same; no new targets. It cannot free previous nums to cover other targets because previous nums already processed and their coverage is fixed in dp[p]. But dp[p] is min cost to cover p using previous nums; it doesn't store alternative coverage of other targets. If current num covers some targets in p, then previous nums didn't need to cover them; that would correspond to a different old mask p' (p minus overlap), and previous nums could have covered other targets? Wait previous nums processed before current num; in the actual solution, previous nums might cover a set p' that is not a subset of p? Let's think.

Our DP state dp[p] means "minimum cost using processed nums to cover exactly/at least mask p"? We treat dp[mask] as cost to cover at least mask? Actually we update dp[mask|s] and never remove bits, so dp[mask] is min cost to cover all bits in mask (possibly more? But we store by exact OR; if a solution covers more than mask, it would be stored at larger mask, not at mask. However, dp[mask] could also be achieved by covering extra bits? No, update stores OR result, so if extra bits covered, mask would include them. So dp[mask] is exact mask covered, not at least. But for monotonicity, dp[p'] <= dp[p] if p' subset p? Is that true for exact mask? If a solution covers p exactly, it also covers p' (as a subset) but the cost is same; dp[p'] is min cost to cover exactly p', which could be less or equal because the same solution covers p' (and extra bits p\p'). Wait if the solution covers p exactly, it covers p' plus extra bits. dp[p'] allows covering exactly p', so it can use the same nums and maybe not require extra bits; cost same. Thus dp[p'] <= dp[p]. Good. So overlapping transition dominated.

But consider if previous nums in dp[p] cover p exactly, and current num covers overlap s, final mask p|s = p (if s subset p) or p plus new. Let overlap o = s ∩ p, new bits n = s \ p. The old mask p includes o. If current num covers o and n, then previous nums didn't need to cover o; they could have covered p \ o instead, with cost dp[p \ o] <= dp[p]. Then current num covers s (which includes o and n), final mask (p\o) ∪ s = p ∪ n = p|s. Since s disjoint from p\o? s includes o, and p\o excludes o, so disjoint. Cost dp[p\o]+cost[s] <= dp[p]+cost[s]. Thus disjoint transition from p\o to p|s dominates. Good. So restricting disjoint safe.

It cannot free previous nums to cover other targets because previous nums already processed and their coverage is fixed in dp[p]. But dp[p] is min cost to cover p using previous nums; it doesn't store alternative coverage of other targets. If current num covers some targets in p, then previous nums didn't need to cover them; that would correspond to a different old mask p' (p minus overlap), and previous nums could have covered other targets? Our DP state dp[p] means "minimum cost using processed nums to cover exactly/at least mask p"? We treat dp[mask] as cost to cover at least mask? Actually we update dp[mask|s] and never remove bits, so dp[mask] is min cost to cover all bits in mask (possibly more? But we store by exact OR; if a solution covers more than mask, it would be stored at larger mask, not at mask. However, dp[mask] could also be achieved by covering extra bits? No, update stores OR result, so if extra bits covered, mask would include them. So dp[mask] is exact mask covered, not at least. But for monotonicity, dp[p'] <= dp[p] if p' subset p? Is that true for exact mask? If a solution covers p exactly, it also covers p' (as a subset) but the cost is same; dp[p'] is min cost to cover exactly p', which could be less or equal because the same solution covers p' (and extra bits p\p'). Wait if the solution covers p exactly, it covers p' plus extra bits. dp[p'] allows covering exactly p', so it can use the same nums and maybe not require extra bits; cost same. Thus dp[p'] <= dp[p]. Good. So overlapping transition dominated.

But consider if previous nums in dp[p] cover p exactly, and current num covers overlap s, final mask p|s = p (if s subset p) or p plus new. Let overlap o = s ∩ p, new bits n = s \ p. The old mask p includes o. If current num covers o and n, then previous nums didn't need to cover o; they could have covered p \ o instead, with cost dp[p \ o] <= dp[p]. Then current num covers s (which includes o and n), final mask (p\o) ∪ s = p ∪ n = p|s. Since s disjoint from p\o? s includes o, and p\o excludes o, so disjoint. Cost dp[p\o]+cost[s] <= dp[p]+cost[s]. Thus disjoint transition from p\o to p|s dominates. Good. So restricting disjoint safe.

Before final, let's consider if there is a possible bug in old-copy DP with disjoint transitions and `trans[mask]` precomputed as nonempty subsets of remaining targets. Suppose old mask p has some targets covered. Current num could cover a subset s that includes targets already covered, and by doing so, it could allow previous nums to cover a different subset that was not covered before? No, previous nums decisions fixed in dp[p]. If current num covers overlap, final mask same; no new targets. It cannot free previous nums to cover other targets because previous nums already processed and their coverage is fixed in dp[p]. But dp[p] is min cost to cover p using previous nums; it doesn't store alternative coverage of other targets. If current num covers some targets in p, then previous nums didn't need to cover them; that would correspond to a different old mask p' (p minus overlap), and previous nums could have covered other targets? Wait previous nums processed before current num; in the actual solution, previous nums might cover a set p' that is not a subset of p? Let's think.

Our DP state dp[p] means "minimum cost using processed nums to cover exactly/at least mask p"? We treat dp[mask] as cost to cover at least mask? Actually we update dp[mask|s] and never remove bits, so dp[mask] is min cost to cover all bits in mask (possibly more? But we store by exact OR; if a solution covers more than mask, it would be stored at larger mask, not at mask. However, dp[mask] could also be achieved by covering extra bits? No, update stores OR result, so if extra bits covered, mask would include them. So dp[mask] is exact mask covered, not at least. But for monotonicity, dp[p'] <= dp[p] if p' subset p? Is that true for exact mask? If a solution covers p exactly, it also covers p' (as a subset) but the cost is same; dp[p'] is min cost to cover exactly p', which could be less or equal because the same solution covers p' (and extra bits p\p'). Wait if the solution covers p exactly, it covers p' plus extra bits. dp[p'] allows covering exactly p', so it can use the same nums and maybe not require extra bits; cost same. Thus dp[p'] <= dp[p]. Good. So overlapping transition dominated.

But consider if previous nums in dp[p] cover p exactly, and current num covers overlap s, final mask p|s = p (if s subset p) or p plus new. Let overlap o = s ∩ p, new bits n = s \ p. The old mask p includes o. If current num covers o and n, then previous nums didn't need to cover o; they could have covered p \ o instead, with cost dp[p \ o] <= dp[p]. Then current num covers s (which includes o and n), final mask (p\o) ∪ s = p ∪ n = p|s. Since s disjoint from p\o? s includes o, and p\o excludes o, so disjoint. Cost dp[p\o]+cost[s] <= dp[p]+cost[s]. Thus disjoint transition from p\o to p|s dominates. Good. So restricting disjoint safe.

Before final, let's consider if there is a possible bug in old-copy DP with disjoint transitions and `trans[mask]` precomputed as nonempty subsets of remaining targets. Suppose old mask p has some targets covered. Current num could cover a subset s that includes targets already covered, and by doing so, it could allow previous nums to cover a different subset that was not covered before? No, previous nums decisions fixed in dp[p]. If current num covers overlap, final mask same; no new targets. It cannot free previous nums to cover other targets because previous nums already processed and their coverage is fixed in dp[p]. But dp[p] is min cost to cover p using previous nums; it doesn't store alternative coverage of other targets. If current num covers some targets in p, then previous nums didn't need to cover them; that would correspond to a different old mask p' (p minus overlap), and previous nums could have covered other targets? Wait previous nums processed before current num; in the actual solution, previous nums might cover a set p' that is not a subset of p? Let's think.

Our DP state dp[p] means "minimum cost using processed nums to cover exactly/at least mask p"? We treat dp[mask] as cost to cover at least mask? Actually we update dp[mask|s] and never remove bits, so dp[mask] is min cost to cover all bits in mask (possibly more? But we store by exact OR; if a solution covers more than mask, it would be stored at larger mask, not at mask. However, dp[mask] could also be achieved by covering extra bits? No, update stores OR result, so if extra bits covered, mask would include them. So dp[mask] is exact mask covered, not at least. But for monotonicity, dp[p'] <= dp[p] if p' subset p? Is that true for exact mask? If a solution covers p exactly, it also covers p' (as a subset) but the cost is same; dp[p'] is min cost to cover exactly p', which could be less or equal because the same solution covers p' (and extra bits p\p'). Wait if the solution covers p exactly, it covers p' plus extra bits. dp[p'] allows covering exactly p', so it can use the same nums and maybe not require extra bits; cost same. Thus dp[p'] <= dp[p]. Good. So overlapping transition dominated.

But consider if previous nums in dp[p] cover p exactly, and current num covers overlap s, final mask p|s = p (if s subset p) or p plus new. Let overlap o = s ∩ p, new bits n = s \ p. The old mask p includes o. If current num covers o and n, then previous nums didn't need to cover o; they could have covered p \ o instead, with cost dp[p \ o] <= dp[p]. Then current num covers s (which includes o and n), final mask (p\o) ∪ s = p ∪ n = p|s. Since s disjoint from p\o? s includes o, and p\o excludes o, so disjoint. Cost dp[p\o]+cost[s] <= dp[p]+cost[s]. Thus disjoint transition from p\o to p|s dominates. Good. So restricting disjoint safe.

It cannot free previous nums to cover other targets because previous nums already processed and their coverage is fixed in dp[p]. Is that true for exact mask? If a solution covers p exactly, it also covers p' (as a subset) but the cost is same; dp[p'] is min cost to cover exactly p', which could be less or equal because the same solution covers p' (and extra bits p\p'). Wait if the solution covers p exactly, it covers p' plus extra bits. dp[p'] allows covering exactly p', so it can use the same nums and maybe not require extra bits; cost same. Thus dp[p'] <= dp[p]. Good. So overlapping transition dominated.

But consider if previous nums in dp[p] cover p exactly, and current num covers overlap s, final mask p|s = p (if s subset p) or p plus new. Let overlap o = s ∩ p, new bits n = s \ p. The old mask p includes o. If current num covers o and n, then previous nums didn't need to cover o; they could have covered p \ o instead, with cost dp[p \ o] <= dp[p]. Then current num covers s (which includes o and n), final mask (p\o) ∪ s = p ∪ n = p|s. Since s disjoint from p\o? s includes o, and p\o excludes o, so disjoint. Cost dp[p\o]+cost[s] <= dp[p]+cost[s]. Thus disjoint transition from p\o to p|s dominates. Good. So restricting disjoint safe.

Now, let's think if there is any issue with "dp[mask]" exact vs at least in our DP. We initialize dp[0]=0. When processing nums, from mask p and subset s disjoint, update p|s. === ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List
from math import gcd

class Solution:
    def minimumIncrements(self, nums: List[int], target: List[int]) -> int:
        k = len(target)
        full = (1 << k) - 1
        M = 1 << k

        # lcm[mask] = lcm of target values indicated by mask
        lcm = [1] * M
        for mask in range(1, M):
            bit = mask & -mask
            j = bit.bit_length() - 1
            prev = mask ^ bit
            lcm[mask] = lcm[prev] // gcd(lcm[prev], target[j]) * target[j]

        # For each already-covered mask, enumerate non-empty subsets of remaining targets.
        trans = [[] for _ in range(M)]
        for mask in range(M):
            rem = full ^ mask
            s = rem
            while s:
                trans[mask].append((s, mask | s))
                s = (s - 1) & rem

        INF = 10 ** 30
        dp = [INF] * M
        dp[0] = 0

        for num in nums:
            cost = [0] * M
            for mask in range(1, M):
                L = lcm[mask]
                cost[mask] = (L - num % L) % L

            new_dp = dp[:]
            for mask in range(M):
                base = dp[mask]
                if base >= INF:
                    continue
                for s, nm in trans[mask]:
                    val = base + cost[s]
                    if val < new_dp[nm]:
                        new_dp[nm] = val

            dp = new_dp
            if dp[full] == 0:
                return 0

        return dp[full]
```
